I mean, sometimes meetings or church briefings can be boring or a little stressful like anything else, but it’s more like… my body just doesn’t want to stay parked.\n\nLike I’ll be sitting there and it feels almost like there’s a little motor humming under my skin, if that makes sense.   },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I guess if I’m being honest, it has caused some real problems at work. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, because I do try hard and I’m not trying to be disrespectful.

But there have been complaints, or corrections, when I jump in too fast and miss part of what somebody was telling me. During room turnovers or when we’re deciding supplies, it can create friction because I think I know what’s needed and I move ahead, and then it turns out I didn’t hear the whole thing.

My supervisor actually suggested I talk to the employee assistance program. So… yeah, I suppose it’s more than just annoying sometimes. Is that the kind of problem you mean?

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n8\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.1, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, welcome in—thanks for coming today. To start, can you tell me what's been bothering you or what brought you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess what’s been getting to me is… I have a hard time just sitting still. I don’t mean to make it sound worse than it is, but it’s like there’s a little motor running under my skin when I’m supposed to stay put—like during staff meetings or briefings at church events.\n\nI’ll start shifting around, or I’ll stand up, or find some reason to go do something. I’m not trying to be difficult or anything… it’s just uncomfortable sitting there too long. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you started or changed the dose of any new medications recently, especially antipsychotics, anti-nausea drugs, or antidepressants, around the time this restlessness began?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I haven’t started anything like that recently—not that I can remember, anyway. No new medicine or dose change around when this started. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Thinking back, did you notice this same kind of restlessness or difficulty sitting still when you were a child or teenager, or is this something that started more recently in adulthood?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess if I’m being honest, it wasn’t just recent. I remember back in grade school getting in trouble for popping up out of my seat, or talking over the teacher. Sometimes I’d rush ahead before they’d even finished giving the directions.\n\nMy mom used to say I was “wound too tight,” even as a kid. I don’t want to make it sound like I was a terrible kid or anything, but… yeah, that same kind of restlessness was there pretty early on.\n\nDoctor: Besides the restlessness, do you often have trouble staying focused on tasks, following through on details, or find your mind wandering during conversations or work?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure I’d say that’s a big problem for me. I mean, everybody’s mind wanders sometimes, right? But I don’t know that I’ve noticed it enough to call it a main issue.\n\nFor me it’s more… I know when I’m uncomfortable staying put. But with focus or details, I’d have to think about that more. I don’t want to give you a wrong answer. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: When you feel that restlessness and need to move, is it usually paired with worrying thoughts or a sense of dread about something, or does it happen even when your mind isn't particularly worried about anything?\n\nPatient: It’s not usually because I’m worried, I don’t think. I mean, sometimes meetings or church briefings can be boring or a little stressful like anything else, but it’s more like… my body just doesn’t want to stay parked.\n\nLike I’ll be sitting there and it feels almost like there’s a little motor humming under my skin, if that makes sense. I start shifting around, or I’ll stand up, or find some reason to go check on something. I’m not trying to be difficult or anything—it just gets uncomfortable to sit still too long. Is that the kind of difference you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting people, blurting out answers before someone finishes a question, or having trouble waiting your turn in conversations or lines?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I try not to be rude about it, but I probably do that more than I should.\n\nLike if my supervisor, or someone from the church staff, starts giving me instructions, I’ll jump in with what I think they’re about to say before they’ve finished. In my head I’m just trying to show I understand, you know? But sometimes I’m wrong, and then they have to stop and correct me.\n\nI don’t mean to cut people off. It just kind of comes out before I’ve fully let them finish. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Would you say these patterns of restlessness and jumping in before others finish have caused real problems for you—like at work, at home, or in your relationships—not just occasional annoyance?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess if I’m being honest, it has caused some real problems at work. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, because I do try hard and I’m not trying to be disrespectful.\n\nBut there have been complaints, or corrections, when I jump in too fast and miss part of what somebody was telling me. During room turnovers or when we’re deciding supplies, it can create friction because I think I know what’s needed and I move ahead, and then it turns out I didn’t hear the whole thing.\n\nMy supervisor actually suggested I talk to the employee assistance program. So… yeah, I suppose it’s more than just annoying sometimes. Is that the kind of problem you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you notice this same restlessness and jumping in before others finish happening at home or in your personal relationships too, not just at work or church?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I mean… at home I don’t think anyone has really sat me down and said, “You’re doing this,” if that’s what you mean.\n\nI probably do get a little antsy if I’m sitting too long, and I might jump in sometimes when someone’s talking, but I’ve mostly noticed it being a problem at work or in church settings where there are instructions and other people depending on me. At home it feels less formal, so maybe it doesn’t stand out as much.\n\nDoes that answer it the way you’re asking?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Yeah… I think I do, if I’m paying attention to it. I might not call it “restless” exactly, but I don’t really settle very well.

I don’t know if that’s what you mean, but yes, it’s not only at work or church. It shows up at home too, just maybe in a way that seems more useful, so I don’t always think of it as a problem.

I start shifting around, or I’ll stand up, or find some reason to go check on something. I’m not trying to be difficult or anything—it just gets uncomfortable to sit still too long. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Would you say these patterns of restlessness and jumping in before others finish have caused real problems for you—like at work, at home, or in your relationships—not just occasional annoyance?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess if I’m being honest, it has caused some real problems at work. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, because I do try hard and I’m not trying to be disrespectful.\n\nBut there have been complaints, or corrections, when I jump in too fast and miss part of what somebody was telling me. During room turnovers or when we’re deciding supplies, it can create friction because I think I know what’s needed and I move ahead, and then it turns out I didn’t hear the whole thing.\n\nMy supervisor actually suggested I talk to the employee assistance program. So… yeah, I suppose it’s more than just annoying sometimes. Is that the kind of problem you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you notice this same restlessness and jumping in before others finish happening at home or in your personal relationships too, not just at work or church?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I mean… at home I don’t think anyone has really sat me down and said, “You’re doing this,” if that’s what you mean.\n\nI probably do get a little antsy if I’m sitting too long, and I might jump in sometimes when someone’s talking, but I’ve mostly noticed it being a problem at work or in church settings where there are instructions and other people depending on me. At home it feels less formal, so maybe it doesn’t stand out as much.\n\nDoes that answer it the way you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Even if no one has pointed it out, do you personally notice yourself feeling restless or interrupting people when you're at home or with family and friends, not just at work or church?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I do, if I’m paying attention to it. Is that the kind of difference you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting people, blurting out answers before someone finishes a question, or having trouble waiting your turn in conversations or lines?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I try not to be rude about it, but I probably do that more than I should.\n\nLike if my supervisor, or someone from the church staff, starts giving me instructions, I’ll jump in with what I think they’re about to say before they’ve finished. In my head I’m just trying to show I understand, you know? But sometimes I’m wrong, and then they have to stop and correct me.\n\nI don’t mean to cut people off. It just kind of comes out before I’ve fully let them finish. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Would you say these patterns of restlessness and jumping in before others finish have caused real problems for you—like at work, at home, or in your relationships—not just occasional annoyance?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess if I’m being honest, it has caused some real problems at work. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, because I do try hard and I’m not trying to be disrespectful.\n\nBut there have been complaints, or corrections, when I jump in too fast and miss part of what somebody was telling me. During room turnovers or when we’re deciding supplies, it can create friction because I think I know what’s needed and I move ahead, and then it turns out I didn’t hear the whole thing.\n\nMy supervisor actually suggested I talk to the employee assistance program. So… yeah, I suppose it’s more than just annoying sometimes. Is that the kind of problem you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you notice this same restlessness and jumping in before others finish happening at home or in your personal relationships too, not just at work or church?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I mean… at home I don’t think anyone has really sat me down and said, “You’re doing this,” if that’s what you mean.\n\nI probably do get a little antsy if I’m sitting too long, and I might jump in sometimes when someone’s talking, but I’ve mostly noticed it being a problem at work or in church settings where there are instructions and other people depending on me. At home it feels less formal, so maybe it doesn’t stand out as much.\n\nDoes that answer it the way you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Even if no one has pointed it out, do you personally notice yourself feeling restless or interrupting people when you're at home or with family and friends, not just at work or church?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I do, if I’m paying attention to it. I might not call it “restless” exactly, but I don’t really settle very well.\n\nLike after a shift, instead of just sitting down, I’ll end up puttering in the garage or re-sorting tools, or finding some little thing to straighten up. And at work it’s kind of the same—I’m going from mopping to checking trash to straightening chairs, without really stopping much.\n\nI don’t know if that’s what you mean, but yes, it’s not only at work or church. It shows up at home too, just maybe in a way that seems more useful, so I don’t always think of it as a problem.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the restlessness and interrupting, have you had ongoing problems with things like breaking rules, getting into fights, being defiant toward authority, or other conduct issues at work, school, or in your community?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve had corrections at work, like I said, but not for fighting or breaking rules or being defiant on purpose.\n\nI try to be respectful, maybe sometimes I come off too quick or I don’t slow down enough, but I’m not trying to challenge anybody’s authority. I haven’t had trouble with the law or anything like that. At least… nothing I’d think of as conduct problems. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often lose track of things like your keys, phone, or paperwork, or find yourself forgetting appointments or tasks you meant to do?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure… I wouldn’t say it’s a big pattern that I’ve noticed. I mean, I might misplace something now and then like anybody does, but I don’t know that I’m always losing keys or forgetting appointments.\n\nWith tasks, I try to keep things pretty routine so I don’t drop the ball. If I do forget something, I usually feel bad about it and try to fix it quick. But I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Thinking back to before you were about 12 years old, did this restlessness and impulsiveness already cause you noticeable trouble—like getting in trouble at school or friction with family—or did that start a bit later?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it was there pretty early, honestly. I don’t want to make it sound like I was a terrible kid or anything, but in grade school I do remember getting in trouble for popping out of my seat or talking when the teacher was still talking.\n\nAnd I’d rush ahead before the directions were finished sometimes, like I thought I already knew what to do. My mom used to say I was “wound too tight,” even when I was little. So… yes, I guess it did start before 12, at least some of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble organizing tasks or following through on multi-step instructions at work, even when you're not feeling restless—like missing steps or making careless mistakes because you didn't fully process what was asked?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that happens often, no. At work I’ve kind of learned to stick to a routine, and if someone gives me steps, I usually try to repeat it back or just get started carefully.\n\nI mean, I’m sure I’ve missed a detail here or there, but I don’t know that it’s a big thing for me when I’m not feeling keyed up. I wouldn’t want to overstate it. Maybe if the instructions are long, I might ask again just to make sure I’ve got it right—is that what you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world. Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health. Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank. Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight. Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event. Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings). Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health. Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).   }

Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry. Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.
